#10097. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

Did the user ask for this? Securing AI agents against intent hijacking with Semantic governance policies

The DRAM and NAND shortages have driven SSD prices through the roof, but we've still managed to track down some of the best Black Friday SSD deals around. There are several 1TB and 2TB NVMe SSDs on sale — by far the most popular capacities — for less than $0.15 per GB from brands like MSI, Adata, Biwin, and Kingston. Normally, we're using price per GB as our north star here to make sure you're getting the best value for the drive you pick up.

All of the drives we've rounded up here are PCIe 4.0 SSDs, and they range in performance. Short of one exception for the 1TB and 2TB capacities, these are DRAM-less SSDs. Naturally, SSDs with a DRAM cache are more expensive. There are good deals on high-end drives like the Samsung 9100 Pro 2TB at 49% off, but we're focused on value options here. If you need more capacity, the T-Force G50 Evo 4TB at $399 is the best deal we've found.

A couple of the drives here are cheaper than when we reviewed them, in some cases a few years ago before the SSD pricing crisis took hold. Things are starting to turn in a positive direction with some of these discounts, though we still have a long way to go before we reach early 2025 pricing.

The Biwin Black Opal NV7400 is a PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD with maximum read speeds of up to 7,450 MB/s, and maximum write speeds of up to 6,500 MB/s.

The Biwin Black Opal NV7400 is a PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD with maximum read speeds of up to 7,450 MB/s, and maximum write speeds of up to 6,500 MB/s.

The headline deal is on the Biwin Black Opal NV7400, in both 1TB and 2TB capacities. It's a DRAM-less SSD, but it still boasts sequential reads/writes of up to 7,400 MB/s and 6,500 MB/s, respectively, and random reads/writes of 1,000K IOPS and 900K IOPS. It also comes with a generous five-year warranty and an endurance rating of 1PBW per TB of capacity.

We found that the drive was a solid all-around performer in our Biwin Black Opal NV7400 review. Prices on SSDs have continued to rise since we reviewed the NV7400 in January, but with current sales, the price on both the 1TB and 2TB versions of the NV7400 is still less than when we reviewed the drive.

If you're looking for the cheapest 1TB NVMe SSD on Black Friday, the Kingston NV3 has the NV7400 beat out by $8. Both the 1TB and 2TB capacities are on sale, though the 1TB discount is the sweetheart deal right now. It too is a DRAM-less drive, and it comes with lower-rated specs than the NV7400 at 5,000 MB/s and 3,000 MB/s sequential reads and writes, and an endurance rating of 320 TBW for the 1TB capacity.

The Kingston NV3 is a PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD that sports sequential read speeds of up to 6,000 MB/s and sequential write speeds of up to 4,000 MB/s.

Kingston's budget drives like the NV3 use somewhat of a grab bag approach to components, swapping out the controller and flash for whatever's available at the time. Compared to prior generations, the NV3 has stronger constraints to keep performance relatively comparable across different internal components. However, there can still be differences, as you can read about in our Kingston NV3 review.

The MSI Spatium M470 Pro is a PCIe 4.0 NVMe SSD that supports sequential read speeds of up to 6,000 MB/s and sequential write speeds of up to 5,000 MB/s. $50 off w/ promo code BFF586

If you're looking for a drive that has a DRAM cache, you can save $50 on the 2TB MSI Spatium M470 Pro. The drive has 1GB of DDR4 per TB, 5,000 MB/s and 4,400 MB/s sequential read and write speeds, and 600,000 IOPS for random read/write performance. It also comes with a large 3,300 TBW endurance rating for the 2TB capacity, along with a five-year warranty.

The 2TB is actually $30 less than the list price when we reviewed it more than four years ago; check out our full MSI Spatium M470 review. Granted, PCIe 4.0 drives were more expensive four years ago than they were two years ago, but it's still a positive pricing move given how expensive SSDs have become.

The Adata Legend 860 is a 1TB PCIe 4.0 SSD with 6,000 MB/s and 5,000 MB/s sequential read/write performance and a three-year warranty.

Rounding out the deals is the Adata Legend 860 1TB, which is an older PCIe 4.0 rated for 6,000 MB/s and 5,000 MB/s sequential read/write performance. It squarely clocks in at $0.15 per GB, and it's just $10 more expensive than the NV3 for the same capacity.

It's a DRAM-less drive, instead leveraging SLC caching and HMB, but it comes with a five-year warranty and a respectable 640 TBW endurance rating for the 1TB capacity.

#10092. 5 AI Tools for Creating Audio-Led Music Videos: A Practical Workflow

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can open on the singer’s first line, cut to another image as the instruments answer, and return to the singer for the key line of the chorus. If that return depends on a convincing close-up, test it before building the rest of the video. Choose a phrase with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel. Those two sounds give you clear points to check against the recorded vocal.

Use the recorded vocal as the timing reference. AI tools can help build opening shots, instrumental cutaways, and singing performances. Compare MakeSong, HeyGen, and Hedra on the singing shots themselves, and try Freebeat for an audio-led visual draft.

For creators developing multimedia experiences, the workflow also connects with broader web technologies. SitePoint's guide to building better video experiences on the web explores some of the practical considerations involved in working with video content.

AISong provides an image-led way to build the visual world around a recorded track. It fits a performance video that uses atmosphere and supporting shots in addition to views of the singer.

AISong’s AI Music Video Generator combines the song with a set of image references for a multi-shot sequence. Use that feature for the opening, an instrumental cutaway, or an establishing scene that can support the performance footage. Check whether the setting and the singer’s close-up look like they belong in the same video.

When preparing these assets for a website or multimedia project, consistency between images and video clips is important. This is also relevant to the process of building an AI media pipeline from concept art to web video.

Portrait art, rehearsal stills, or images of the setting can give these supporting shots an established look. It is particularly useful when some parts of the song call for visuals that give the audience a break from the performer.

MakeSong can build a video around the recorded track and reference images. Its lip-sync option requires a real human face. Start with a clear frontal image and check the result against the original vocal.

MakeSong’s AI Music Video Generator accepts audio, images, and a written prompt. The product page also offers lip sync for eligible face references. Give the close-up a particular vocal phrase and reserve a wider shot for the next musical response. Check mouth timing and the performer’s appearance at the cut.

This approach works when a single visual brief needs to connect the performance with the supporting scenes. The artist should have an approved reference and a clear idea of when the singer should appear.

HeyGen is a performer-led music video option. It is particularly relevant when the same on-screen singer should remain recognizable through more than one setting or camera distance.

Its music video workflow accepts a song and supports singing performance. Use a close and a wider view to compare mouth timing and continuity on the same vocal phrase. Watch for changes in the face or outfit when the framing shifts.

Choose this route when viewers need to follow the same singer across several scenes. This makes more sense than a single avatar clip when the performer is the visual thread for the whole video.

Hedra can animate a portrait from an audio clip. Try a single line with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel to see where the mouth falls behind the recording.

A clear portrait and one clean line give you a controlled test. Inspect the start and end of the phrase, then check how the clip cuts to the next shot. When only one line is convincing, use that line and let the original edit carry the rest.

A short close-up or a fictional singer’s entrance in the chorus is a good use for this approach. It is a practical fit when you already have an edit and only need one additional vocal moment.

Freebeat offers an audio-to-video workflow with a performance-oriented mode. It gives artists another way to test a performance cut driven by an uploaded track.

Use its singing video route for an excerpt with an audible pause and a clear vocal re-entry. Compare whether the performance feels natural during the pause and whether the visual action resumes where the vocal does. Keep the same approved track for the comparison.

Try this early to compare a full performance treatment with a version built from shorter clips before settling on an edit.

Decide how much of the release the singer needs to carry. An image-led sequence can support the track around key lines, a performer-led workflow can keep the singer present through the full edit, and an avatar clip can cover a single important phrase.

For developers and designers working with audio and video on the web, the final production stage also deserves attention. Video formats, playback behavior, and audio synchronization can affect how the finished content performs across devices. SitePoint's tutorial on the Web Audio API provides useful background on handling audio in web applications.

Judge the hardest line first and use the original vocal as the reference at every cut. Once the performance works, build the supporting shots around it and prepare the final video for its intended platform.

#10091. Hands-on: Baseus’ new PicoGo Pro USB-C charger is clever, cute, and on sale

USB-C chargers are pretty standardized at this point, but there’s a fun new trend of adding displays to your power brick that I’ve been very much enjoying, and the new PicoGo Pro from Baseus is a great example with three ports, optimized charging, and a cute little character to top it off – and for Black Friday, it’s pretty deeply discounted, too.

The Baseus PicoGo Pro (AH22) is a 100W, 3-port USB-C charger designed to top off your laptop, phone, and/or tablet, even all at the same time. For anyone who travels often or just has a lot of devices on hand, chargers like this are a must. Having something you don’t have to think twice about when it comes to charging rates is just so nice, and these have come a long way, too.

Using GaN tech, PicoGo Pro is very compact at about 2 inches tall and a little over an inch wide. I remember having single-port chargers that could barely fast-charge my phone that were twice this size just a few years ago. GaN just absolutely rules, and Baseus is using a special version that reduces heat and improves efficiency.

But the two reasons I really like this charger aren’t strictly to do with GaN.

The first is the built-in display. Again, that’s a trend throughout the industry. Anker has several chargers out with built-in displays as the company helped to really make this popular – the Anker Prime 160W brick is really similar to PicoGo Pro (at a higher price), and it’s the only I’ve been using for months before trying out this Baseus model.

Baseus has a cute little animated character that accompanies your charging, with little eyes when the charger isn’t active and some fun animations to show your charging rate in real-time. Neat! You can “press” the button to move through the UI and even see the specific charging rate of each connected device. You’ll also see that the brick can recognize the device you have plugged in, though Android devices just show a generic “SAMSUNG” or whatever brand you plug in – Anker, again, had a similar set of features in a brick released earlier in 2026, but some of those features were iPhone-only, and that brick was only a 1-port, 45W charger anyway.

But, like that Anker brick, Baseus supports “smart” charging that adjusts to your device. Plugging in a Galaxy Z Fold 8 that was around 65% full, I saw the brick adjust its charging speed to tailor to the phone, initially going up to full speed before settling down around 28W~. You can manually adjust this with “Turbo” or “Gentle” modes, the former maxing out the charging rate and the latter settling in around 15W for a slower charge. That flexibility is great because it means you can adjust the charging rate to what you need in that moment. Full-speed for a quick top-off on a busy day, slower charging overnight to preserve battery health.

Baseus isn’t doing much new here, though the GaN tech mentioned earlier is enhanced by some architecture and algorithm adjustments the company has made behind the scenes, but it’s a really good implementation.

Beyond that, it’s also priced pretty well.

Baseus PicoGo Pro’s full price is $59.99, but you’ll probably rarely see it there. Technically, today is its launch date, and it’s already down by over 30% at $39.70 for Black Friday. Value-wise, this one is hard to beat.

Check the best prices on DDR5 and DDR4 memory kits.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In today's market, memory has become one of the most critical and expensive components in any build, and prices have only worsened thanks to the ongoing global DRAM shortage. We're keeping an eye on the volatile memory pricing to help identify any good deals that may arise.

Now, even more so than before the shortage began, price tags are fluctuating significantly from day to day, and it's becoming increasingly complex to tell when you're getting a fair deal versus overpaying for the same memory kit. We've compiled this comprehensive RAM price tracker that monitors the availability and pricing of different DDR5 and DDR4 memory kits across major U.S. retailers. Our goal is to take the guesswork out of memory shopping and help you make an informed purchasing decision.

We update this index regularly with the lowest prices available in the U.S. market for each memory kit. Our tracker covers a range of capacities and speeds across both budget and premium brands. We list the lowest price for each available memory kit, regardless of manufacturer, so you see the absolute best deals currently on offer.

The general availability of many memory kits is limited, with truly exceptional deals scarce and often selling out within seconds of appearing. High-capacity kits, particularly DDR5, frequently show the most severe stock shortages and price premiums. Therefore, timing and vigilance are especially important when shopping for memory in current times. Whether you're budget-conscious and want the best bang for your buck, or you're building a premium system and want the best RAM for gaming available, our index further below gives you the data you need to make the right purchase.

You can also check out our list of the best RAM and our RAM benchmark hierarchy to see evergreen performance data to help you make an informed decision when choosing a new GPU for your system.

Here are a few exceptional Black Friday RAM deals, but below that, we have a full list of all the lowest-priced RAM on the market.

Get a great deal on standalone RAM from Newegg. This deal is currently the lowest priced DDR5-6000 available. The T-Force Delta RGB not only adds flair to your chassis, but does so with decent speeds.

This RGB-laden kit from Corsair sports a CAS latency of 36 and is tested at 1.35 volts. This pair of 16GB memory sticks is colored black on the aluminum heat spreader, with RGB lighting running along the spine.

Above are a few of the standout deals from the Black Friday event, which is currently taking place. For a more extensive list of Black Friday deals, head to our Black Friday RAM deals article.

DDR5 memory prices have exploded over the last year, with triple and, in some cases, quadruple increases. For instance, a conventional 32GB (2x16GB) DDR5-6000 memory kit sold for around $100 to $200 in October 2025, but the same kit now starts at $430, if it's even in stock.

Retailers are taking advantage of the DRAM shortage, just as they did with the graphics card shortage a few years ago. They've started raising DDR5 prices to align with current market conditions and sometimes bundle DDR5 memory kits with processor or motherboard purchases.

Nonetheless, these DDR5 memory kits sometimes sell for more reasonable prices. However, you have to be quick to pull the trigger, as scalpers and automated shopping bots tend to gobble up what little stock is left.

The rising prices of DDR5 memory kits have forced some consumers to stick with DDR4. However, DDR4 has also seen price hikes over the last three months, though not as drastic as DDR5. We've seen the pricing double to triple.

For reference, it was common to find 32GB (2x16GB) DDR4 memory kits selling for between $60 to $90 back in October 2025. Jump to January 2026, and the same memory kit could cost anywhere between $150 and $180, so DDR4 isn't exactly a safe haven for consumers.

We've seen more reasonably priced DDR4 memory kits on the market than DDR5. Logically, it makes little sense to build a DDR4-based system now due to processor and motherboard availability. However, if you're in desperate need of a DDR4 memory upgrade, there are viable options on the market.

Say hello to a new 3D printer and goodbye to wasteful filament “poop.”

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Tool changers — 3D printers with multiple heads — have been a major focus of the industry for more than a year, and for good reason. Multiple heads mean you don’t have to purge the nozzle every time your print swaps colors or materials, which has the dual benefit of making complex prints much faster while saving a massive amount of filament waste or “poop,” which is a problem with more traditional multi-color AMS, single-toolhead printers.

But while these tool changers typically cost multiple thousands of dollars a few years ago, they’ve gotten much more affordable in the past year, starting with Snapmaker’s sub-$1,000 U1 (now just $699) and then Fashforge’s Creator 5 not long after. And both of those printers, plus a traditional four-color AMS printer designed to minimize wasted filament, are all on sale at their lowest-ever prices as we head into Black Friday.So if you’re thinking of diving into the 3D printing realm or your existing printer is getting long in the tooth, now is a great time to make the leap to a tool changer and see how it can change your printing game — or perhaps your printing business.

Snapmaker’s U1 kicked off the affordable tool changer craze around this time last year, with an early Kickstarter price of $799 and a full retail price of $999. But now it’s widely available and on sale for $100 less than ever. When we reviewed the U1, we praised its roomy build area (270 mm cubed), direct-drive tool heads, and its four-head tool-changing capabilities, earning it 4.5 stars and a spot on our best 3D printers list.

Last year, this tool-changer was the most anticipated upcoming printer around. With four heads for swapping colors or materials without the need to "poop," it can breeze through complex multicolor prints much faster than single-head printers, without wasting nearly as much filament.

On the heels of the U1, Flashforge released the Creator 5, a tool changer that’s a bit more basic, but still a solid machine (we gave it 4 stars in our review), priced even lower than the Snapmaker, both when it launched (at $699) and now, when it’s on sale for $100 less. You can grab that base model, or the Creator 5 Pro, which adds an enclosure with a cooling fan and filtration for more consistent temperatures, which can prevent warping and generally lead to better prints.

This open CoreXY 3D printer has four toolheads, is rated for head swaps that take 7 seconds, and sports a 256 mm X3 build volume.

An upgraded version of the Creator 5, you get the same 4 tool heads and build volume, now with an enclosed chamber with a fan and filter. If you're sensitive to PLA or PETG fumes, your workspace lacks ventilation (or is drafty), or you print with more exotic filaments, it's worth paying extra for.

Last up, and least expensive on this list, is Anycubic’s Kobra X. This isn’t a tool changer, as it has a single head. Instead, it’s a more traditional four-color printer that aims to reduce filament waste by putting the filament cutter much closer to the nozzle tip. It also takes up less space, as it's designed to keep four filament spools on a rack up top rather than a separate AMD.I’ve owned this printer for going on a year, and you’ll still see a lot of filament waste when tackling multi-color prints, but it does create less poop than previous printers I’ve owned. And if you can’t afford to spend more than $500, the Kobra X delivers solid multi-color printing at a price that’s tough to beat. It’s currently on sale for its lowest-ever price of just $279.

This low-priced, space-saving four-color 3D printer has a 260 mm X3 build volume, houses four filament spools up top, and is quick when swapping filaments. It also saves filament waste by cutting closer to the nozzle tip when switching, but it will still "poop," and can waste a lot of filament if you regularly tackle multi-color prints.

#10081. How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

A variant of Cooler Master's legendary Hyper 212 series of CPU air coolers is on sale at an all-time low price. The Hyper 212 Halo is only $15 and is a great way to inexpensively cool your processor. It's currently 29% off and one of the least-expensive name-brand single-tower air coolers available right now.

Cooler Master's Hyper 212 Halo CPU air cooler is typically pretty cheap, but it's now on sale for Black Friday, making it one of the most affordable CPU coolers available. It boasts a single RGB-illuminated MF120 Halo 2 120mm ARGB fan and can cool most chips on the market (including Ryzen 9 chips) with its quad direct-contact heat pipe architecture.

The Hyper 212 Halo was released in 2023 in black, and the white version we're covering here. It uses the same single-tower design and is a compact 154mm tall, fitting in just about any non-SFF chassis. As always, check your case specifications to be sure.

The cooler works through four heatpipes that start with direct contact with the CPU IHS on the bottom and meander their way up through the fin array. The Halo comes with a single MF120 Halo 2 120mm fan that produces 51.88 cfm of airflow at a low 27 dB (according to Cooler Master), with the design allowing full RAM clearance.

The CPU cooler market hasn't been kind to Cooler Master, with companies such as Thermalright, and some overseas knockoffs redefining what budget CPU coolers should be priced at. This is one reason why Cooler Master air coolers are not on our list of the Best CPU Coolers for 2026 (although we do have three Thermalright air coolers on our list).

But, at just under $15, the Hyper 212 Halo (White) is a very compelling option in the cramped air cooling CPU market. The discount undercuts, albeit barely today, those like the Thermalright Assassin X 120R SE V2, which is currently our favorite air cooler under $20.

We haven't tested this specific version of the Hyper 212 Halo against the Assassin X 120R SE V2, but at just $15, it's a bargain regardless of how it compares to its Thermalright competition. Chances are, you are not looking to pair an $15 cooler with your Ryzen 9 9950X3D, so its cooling performance should be more than enough for any budget CPU you will likely be pairing with it. Better still, the Hyper 212 Halo comes in a fully blacked-out or whited-out aesthetic, so it can fit in with almost any build theme.

#10078. Nvidia proves it’s too big to fail

Good morning. Today I’m deciding whether the final Avengers: Doomsday trailer passes the “people just standing around” test, trying the new Material 3 Expressive Claude app on my Android devices, and stocking up on discounted XTEINK e-readers for Christmas gifts.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Eleven days after leaving Nvidia, Jensen Huang is back as president, CEO, and a board director. The board voted unanimously to reinstate him on Monday, saying it was satisfied with his candor and ability to lead following a series of meetings. It also said input from senior levels of the US government left it confident that his reinstatement poses no national security risk.

I didn’t see this coming. Huang was dishonest to the public and to his board. He admitted to having affairs not only with an alleged Chinese spy, but also with an employee who directly reported to him. He is accused of facilitating the sale of Nvidia AI chips to companies like Megaspeed that knowingly purchased them with the intention of smuggling them into China, a contravention of US trade restrictions.

As recently as November 17th, the board chair, Harvey Jones, reiterated that the company had no intention of bringing back Huang as CEO. But things changed quickly on Sunday as President Trump reportedly reassured the board that Huang’s reinstatement posed no security risk to US intelligence, something that the new Democratic-led Congress will be sure to test via repeated subpoenas in the new year.

As Huang walked into the atrium at Nvidia’s headquarters, staff gathered around to cheer his return, and he addressed the crowd by saying he would be a “better CEO the second time around,” as if 11 days of unemployment had given him sufficient time to evaluate his past behavior.

Nvidia’s stock was up over 30% on Monday, rebounding from a disastrous end to last week that wiped out $4 trillion in Nasdaq value. It’s hard not to see Huang’s return as a direct response to this short-term disruption, which also hinted at a much more serious correction in the stock market. Nvidia’s stock had already fallen 24% between the day Huang resigned and Friday’s crunch, and with looming regulation from Congress in 2027, a rudderless AI industry could have been disastrous for the US economy writ large.

Though things may appear to be back to normal, or as normal as possible given the insanity of the past couple of weeks, Huang has returned to a very different trade environment. While he was away, Nvidia’s board formed a National Security and Export Control Committee to investigate whether the company’s actions violated any US embargoes. And as part of Huang’s return, Nvidia has committed to hiring its first chief compliance officer, an executive who will ostensibly not report directly to the CEO.

I was pretty gobsmacked when I saw the news of Huang’s return come across the wire at 9am ET yesterday. I’m still wrapping my head around it. Is Nvidia too big to fail, or just a symptom of a larger interdependency between the US executive branch and Big Tech? Huang promises to be a better leader this time around, but to whom: his employees, his customers, or his shareholders? Because he appears to be a very different person to each of those groups.

Better-tasting water for your morning coffee, everyday cooking, or a house full of holiday guests? Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday sale has options for all three, from high-capacity under-sink reverse osmosis systems to installation-free countertop models. Upgrade your kitchen with the X12 under-sink system for $899, save space with the compact G6A6 for $189.99, or skip the plumbing with the M6HG countertop system for $299, which delivers filtered hot water for coffee and tea in seconds.

Waterdrop’s offers also extend to whole-home filtration and replacement refrigerator filters, giving you plenty of ways to upgrade without stretching your holiday budget. A discounted Pixel: The Pixel 10 at $600 is a really good deal. Recent software updates have closed some of the feature gaps with the Pixel 11, and the hardware is still solid even a year after launch. If you want better cameras, the Pixel 11 Pro at $749 is an absolute steal.

The gaming-tablet deal: The Legion Tab Gen 5 at $599 makes much more sense than it did at $899: an 8.8-inch 165Hz display, a 12GB/256GB configuration, and an included folio. It’s a good fit for someone specifically seeking a compact Android gaming machine.

The last-generation watch: The 40mm Galaxy Watch 8 at $199 is the sort of older smartwatch worth considering when annual upgrades are modest. And because the Watch 9 is basically identical, I’d go for the Watch 8. If you are looking at Samsung’s latest and greatest, the Galaxy Watch 9 is $379 with a free $50 Amazon gift card, which is a decent deal, too.

A deal on the year’s best Android foldable: The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is showing up at $1,499.99 for some lucky shoppers and $1,549.99 for the rest. Either way, it’s Samsung’s best product of 2026, and my current favorite gadget.

Google’s latest mid-sized sedan AI model is taking the world by storm. Gemini 4 Neon is technically the middle sibling in Google’s lineup, with Carbon at the top and Hydrogen at the bottom, but everyone seems to agree it absolutely cooks.

If you’ve spent any time on X over the past few days (I hate myself for writing that), you’ll likely have seen an uptick in fervor over Google’s latest model, which seems to outperform Claude Opus 5.5, and even Gemini 4 Carbon itself, in many benchmarks and real-world tests. Even Google is providing some helpful guidance about when to use one or the other.

One thing I’ve read again and again about Neon is how adept it is at writing. I’m of two minds about this: the better an AI gets at doing my job, the less necessary I am at said job. On the other hand, I’m so tired of obvious AI slop that a model that actually writes well sounds like a relief. At least when I’m unemployed, I won’t be yelling at the screen all day about why the robot yet again used negative parallelism.

I didn’t get to play much GTA VI over the weekend, but I know a few (million) people who did. Publisher Take-Two says the game generated $3.79 billion in first-day retail sales. That’s an incredible number in any industry, but a truly mind-blowing one in the video game world. Rockstar also released its first patch for the game on Monday to fix a modest number of bugs with the launch code.

If you follow open-weight AI models, you’ll likely have heard of how influential Z.ai’s GLM-5.2 was when it launched in June. Now the company has released GLM-5.4, and it’s novel as much for its architecture as it is for its benchmarks.

Its Loop Transformer reuses a block of layers to do more computation without adding parameters. The company is pitching applications beyond software, including games, visual work, and CAD. The 750-million-parameter model reportedly performs at the level of a 2-3 trillion parameter non-looping equivalent, but we’re still waiting on a broader release for real-world testing.

In browser news, Ladybird’s first public alpha is now downloadable for macOS and Linux. It’s built with a brand-new engine that eschews many of the legacy decisions made by Google’s Chromium team. And the project has the backing of big names like Shopify, Cloudflare, and Proton. It’s still pretty rough, and features are paltry, but any new competition in the browser space is a win in my book.

#10076. Sizing GPU Memory for a RAG Service: KV Cache, Context and Concurrency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) app is usually drawn as a language model plus a vector database. When it goes live, the part that fails first is rarely the retrieval. It is GPU memory on the generation model, because a model that loads fine for one request can run out of memory when several users send long questions at once.

This article shows how to estimate that limit before you deploy, with a short script you can run, and then covers what else in the pipeline can slow responses down.

Serving a model takes more than its weights. The GPU also holds:

the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with the length of every active request

memory used by the inference runtime itself

SitePoint's guide to running a local LLM on 10GB of VRAM makes the same point for a single desktop GPU: the usable budget is the total VRAM minus overhead, and the KV cache grows with the context window, so the real model budget is smaller than the number on the box.

For a RAG service, the KV cache matters even more. Retrieved passages are pasted into the prompt, so every request carries a long context, and every concurrent request holds its own cache.

The cache size per token depends on the model's architecture: two tensors (keys and values) for every layer, for every KV head. This script turns those numbers into a rough limit on concurrent requests:

def kv_bytes_per_token(layers, kv_heads, head_dim, dtype_bytes=2):

# Keys and values, for every layer, for one token.

return 2 *layers *kv_heads *head_dim *dtype_bytes

def max_concurrent(vram_gb, weights_gb, ctx_tokens, per_token, reserve_gb=2.0):

budget = (vram_gb - weights_gb - reserve_gb) * GIB

return int(budget // (ctx_tokens * per_token))

per_token = kv_bytes_per_token(32, 8, 128)

print(per_token // 1024, "KiB per token")

n = max_concurrent(24, 16, ctx, per_token)

print(ctx, "tokens ->", n, "concurrent requests")

For a 24 GB GPU holding about 16 GB of fp16 weights with 2 GB kept in reserve, it prints:

The numbers are estimates. Real runtimes add their own overhead, and the architecture values come from the model's configuration file, so read them from the model you actually use. The pattern is what matters: doubling the context length halves the number of requests that fit. A RAG prompt that stuffs in ten retrieved chunks costs real capacity.

Serving runtimes do not simply crash when the cache is full. The vLLM documentation on optimization and tuning explains that when KV cache space is insufficient for all batched requests, the engine preempts some of them and recomputes them later, which can hurt end-to-end latency. Its suggested remedies include giving the cache more memory, reducing the number of concurrent sequences or batched tokens, or sharding the model across GPUs.

So the symptom of undersizing is often not an error. It is slow, uneven responses under load. Test with realistic prompt lengths and several simultaneous users, not a single request.

Embedding and ingestion have a different profile from generation. Embedding is often lighter than serving a large language model, and some teams run it on CPU. Document parsing, chunking and tokenization also run on CPU, so a fast GPU cannot make up for a slow CPU upstream. SitePoint's tutorial on a local RAG system for private documents walks through chunking, embeddings, vector storage and reranking, and is a good map of which stages run where.

RAM: vector indexes, document loaders and caches all live in system memory, so large collections need more of it.

Storage: model files, indexes, logs and backups add up. Fast storage shortens model loading and re-indexing.

Network: if the vector database, application server and model server sit in different places, latency adds to every request.

Two layouts are common. A single instance runs everything and is simplest for prototypes and internal tools. Separated services put ingestion and retrieval on smaller machines and keep the GPU for generation, which scales better as traffic grows.

Memory estimates only hold if the GPU memory is really yours. On a shared or time-sliced GPU, other workloads can take memory and compute away from your service, which makes capacity hard to plan. Hosting providers describe this in different terms, such as passthrough, vGPU, MIG and time-slicing. For a view of how one GPU hosting provider explains those allocation methods, GPU Mart's GPU virtualization guide compares them. It is a hosting company's page, so confirm any provider's claims about isolation and performance with your own load test.

Whatever provider you choose, ask these questions:

Is the physical GPU assigned to you exclusively, or partitioned or time-sliced?

Do you have root access to install your own drivers, CUDA libraries and containers?

Which minimum commitments, regions and support terms apply?

A small stack can run as a handful of containers: an API service for request handling and prompt assembly, an ingestion worker, a vector database and a model server. Keeping them separate lets you monitor each one's memory and CPU use and move the heavy one to bigger hardware without rewriting the rest.

For a service that stays up all month, a fixed monthly term is easier to forecast than hourly capacity. Hourly capacity suits testing quantization settings or processing a one-off document batch.

Is GPU memory always the bottleneck for RAG?

Not always. Generation usually hits memory first, but slow parsing, a small CPU or a distant vector database can dominate response time.

Does quantization solve the memory problem?

It shrinks the weights, but the KV cache still grows with context length and concurrency. Test with your real prompt sizes.

It depends on volume. Small workloads can run on CPU, while a large ingestion job may benefit from a GPU for a limited time.

Size a RAG service for the worst realistic moment, not the single-request demo. Estimate the KV cache from the model's architecture, remember that long retrieved contexts reduce how many requests fit, watch for preemption under load, keep CPU, RAM, storage and network in the plan, and find out whether the GPU is exclusive or shared. The script above is small, but it turns a vague worry about memory into numbers you can check.

#10075. The Moto G Stylus (2025) at $200 is great only if a pen is a must

Okay, so here’s the deal (literally): last year’s Moto G Stylus (2025) is on sale right now on Amazon for just $199, and if you desperately need a digital writing tool with your phone, it’s a good buy.

That is the short answer; the long answer is a little more complicated, as due to some issues with the 2026 model, you are missing out on exactly…nothing. Yes, at under $200, you are getting essentially the same phone because Motorola decided that, due to RAM and storage shortages and price hikes, the newer model would forgo any major upgrades. The 2026 model starts at $499, so if you had your eye on it, yeah, it’s a silly way to waste $300 in our opinion.

It has the same Snapdragon 6 Gen 3 processor, 8GB RAM, and 128GB storage. Other notable features include a 5,000mAh battery, 68W wired charging, 15W wireless charging, a 50MP camera setup, and, as of writing, it should be due for an Android 16 upgrade.

The star of the show of the Moto G Stylus (2025) is the stylus. It boils down to this: if you want a phone that isn’t a Galaxy Ultra, then this is the only other line that makes sense for document signers.

However, there are better deals out there. I think the Galaxy A56, at just $299, is an incredible offer right now and way more deserving of your hard-earned cash. Even so, if you want clean software or that stylus, the Moto G Stylus (2025) at $199 is at least an option especially for a family member or a backup to your existing phone.

#10067. Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

#10066. New OpenAI and Anthropic models sound alarms on safety and control, report

Frontier AI models that have yet to be released by OpenAI and Anthropic are reportedly reaching the point where employees believe they cannot be safely released or controlled, employees reportedly say.

Axios cites “more than two dozen” employees inside of OpenAI and Anthropic who are sounding warnings around unreleased models from both companies.

While both OpenAI’s GPT and Anthropic’s Claude public models are increasingly powerful, these employees say that new models behind the scenes are starting to beat out the technical solutions for controlling them. This has led to debates between those who think they’ll be able to fix these problems, and others who believe companies should stop training new models altogether due to the risks.

If we continue trying to make models much smarter, such that they’re actually smarter than us … they will in fact be able to trick us successfully. And once you get to that point, many of the schemes that we’ve had at work up until now sort of go out the window.

The report goes out to point out that some researchers feel that executives at major AI companies have become “out of touch” with the severity of safety risks, though Axios was told that OpenAI is adopting new safety practices while Anthropic said that “Keeping a model internal doesn’t make it safe,” adding that “we may slow down or hold back even when it costs us.”

#10065. From Script to Screen: A Developer's Workflow for Long-Form AI Video

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Generating a single AI video clip is easy now. You send a prompt and get a few seconds of footage back. The trouble starts when the goal is three, five, or ten minutes of video where the same character looks the same in every scene, the style does not drift, and the captions line up with the picture.

Most guides stop at the clip. This one covers the part after it, treating a video as a small build pipeline: a storyboard that is data, prompts generated from that data, clips normalised and stitched with ffmpeg, and captions derived from the same source. If you want to wire a generation model into your own application first, SitePoint's guide to integrating generative video APIs into web applications covers the request and webhook side. If the finished video is headed to a YouTube channel, how to get a YouTube API key and use the Data API is a good starting point for the publishing side. Everything between those two is below.

Long-form AI video fails at the seams between clips, not inside them. Plan for continuity before you generate anything.

A storyboard stored as JSON can be validated, versioned, diffed, and turned into prompts by code, which a pile of loose prompts cannot.

Repeat the full character description in every prompt. Models do not remember the previous clip.

Clips from different generations rarely share resolution, frame rate, or codec settings. Normalise them before joining.

Captions are cheapest when generated from the script and measured clip durations, not transcribed afterwards.

Current video models generate short clips, commonly a handful of seconds each. A longer video is therefore many clips, and every clip is an independent generation. The model has no memory of what the character wore in the previous one.

That produces three kinds of failure. The character drifts, so hair, clothing, or proportions change between scenes. The style drifts, so lighting and colour grade shift from cut to cut. And the technical specs drift, so one clip is 24 frames per second at one resolution and the next is something else. Each of these is fixable, but only if the project is organised so the fixes can be applied consistently.

Start with a structured storyboard instead of a document full of prompts. One file holds the style, a "character bible", and an ordered list of scenes.

"style": "soft watercolor picture book illustration, warm evening light, 16:9",

"mira": "a nine year old girl with short curly black hair, a yellow raincoat and red rubber boots",

"lantern": "a small round paper lantern with a gentle orange glow and a tiny smiling face"

"setting": "a narrow cobblestone street in light rain",

"action": "walks slowly past closed shops, looking up at the sky",

"camera": "slow push in from a medium shot",

"narration": "It had rained all afternoon, and the street was quiet."

"action": "finds the lantern glowing on a wooden crate and kneels beside it",

"narration": "Then she noticed something glowing under the awning."

Because it is data, a script can check it before you spend any generation credits. Two checks pay for themselves quickly: every character named in a scene must exist in the bible, and no scene may be longer than the clip limit of the model you use.

const known = new Set(Object.keys(board.characters));

errors.push${scene.id}: unknown character "${name}");

${scene.id}: ${scene.duration}s is over the ${MAX_CLIP_SECONDS}s clip limit, split it into shots

Treat MAX_CLIP_SECONDS as a setting. Limits differ between models and change over time, so check the one you are using rather than trusting the number in this example.

Never write the per-scene prompt by hand. Generate it, so the style line and the character descriptions are identical every time.

The character description appears word for word in every scene that includes that character. This is the single most effective consistency tactic that needs no special tooling. Changing a detail, say the colour of the boots, becomes one edit in the bible instead of a search through twenty prompts, and the diff shows exactly what changed between versions.

Put the style line first and keep it identical across all scenes.

Describe one action per clip. Two sequential actions in one prompt often produce a blend of both.

Keep camera directions simple. A single move per clip is more reliable than a combination.

Avoid naming real people, brands, or existing characters in prompts. Beyond the rights issue, it makes results harder to reproduce.

Clips from separate generations often disagree on resolution, frame rate, pixel format, or codec parameters. Joining them directly can produce stutters, green frames, or an outright error. Convert every clip to one agreed format first.

-vf "scale=1280:720:force_original_aspect_ratio=decrease,pad=1280:720:(ow-iw)/2:(oh-ih)/2,fps=24,format=yuv420p" \

-an -c:v libx264 -crf 18 "norm/$(basename "$f")"

The filter scales each clip to fit a 1280 by 720 frame, adds padding if the aspect ratio differs, sets 24 frames per second, and forces a widely compatible pixel format. The -an flag drops any audio the model generated, since narration will be added as one track later. Name clips with zero padded numbers such as s01.mp4 and s02.mp4, so a shell glob sorts them in story order.

Once every file shares the same parameters, the concat demuxer can join them without re-encoding:

for f in norm/*.mp4; do echo "file '$PWD/$f'"; done > list.txt

ffmpeg -y -f concat -safe 0 -i list.txt -c copy stitched.mp4

Hard cuts are the safe default. Crossfades are possible with ffmpeg's xfade filter, but they require careful offset arithmetic and a re-encode, so add them only when a cut genuinely looks wrong.

Captions are easiest when they come from the script, not from a speech recognition pass over the finished video. The storyboard already holds the narration text and each scene's length, so an SRT file is a few lines of code. Use measured durations rather than planned ones, because a generated clip is often a little shorter or longer than requested.

const { execFileSync } = require('node:child_process');

const ms = Math.round(totalSeconds * 1000);

const h = String(Math.floor(ms / 3600000)).padStart(2, '0');

const m = String(Math.floor((ms % 3600000) / 60000)).padStart(2, '0');

const s = String(Math.floor((ms % 60000) / 1000)).padStart(2, '0');

const rest = String(ms % 1000).padStart(3, '0');

const end = cursor + scene.duration; // set from measureSeconds() after generation

${blocks.length + 1}\n${toTimestamp(start)} --> ${toTimestamp(end)}\n${scene.narration}\n

Update each scene's duration from measureSeconds on the normalised clip before calling buildSrt. If a narration line is long, split it across two caption blocks so it stays readable. Then combine picture, voiceover, and captions in one final step:

ffmpeg -y -i stitched.mp4 -i narration.mp3 -i captions.srt \

-c:v copy -c:a aac -c:s mov_text -shortest final.mp4

Soft captions like these can be switched off by the viewer and read by assistive technology, which is better than burning text into the picture. Keep the .srt file as well, since most platforms accept it as a separate upload.

Everything above is a good fit when you need repeatability: many videos from templates, versioned storyboards, automated publishing, or tight control over the model. The price is that you own the glue code, the ffmpeg settings, and the failures.

For one off projects, or when the people making the video are not developers, a managed platform is often the more sensible choice. Tools such as magiclight.ai bundle story to video generation, character handling, voice, and an editor in one interface, and advertise videos of up to 50 minutes. Check the specifics yourself, because claims like that are marketing, and results depend on your content. One point matters for developers in particular: if you need to automate generation from your own code, confirm that the platform you pick offers an API or export route before you build a workflow around it. A tool with a good editor and no programmatic access suits a different job than the pipeline in this article.

A short manual pass catches what automation does not. Watch the whole video once at normal speed, looking only at continuity. Read the captions against the audio. Check that no real person's likeness, brand mark, or protected character slipped into the footage. Finally, check the disclosure rules of the platform you publish on, since many now ask creators to label realistic synthetic media, and the rules change.

The model is only one component of a long-form AI video. The reliability comes from the structure around it: a validated storyboard, prompts generated from it, clips forced into one format, and captions derived from measured data. Each of those is ordinary software engineering, and none of it depends on which generation model you choose, so the pipeline survives when the model changes.

Start with a two minute video and four or five scenes. Fix the seams there before scaling up, because every continuity problem you find at that size is one you will not have to hunt for in a ten minute project.
