#10054. Your AI Pipeline Works. But Can You Trust It?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI is becoming a part of almost everything we do in technology today. Whether it's detecting fraudulent transactions, assessing credit risk, forecasting demand, or personalizing customer experiences, organizations are increasingly relying on AI to make faster and smarter decisions.

And naturally, when we talk about building AI systems, most of the conversation revolves around model accuracy, performance, and deployment. We spend considerable time making sure our models are trained well, our pipelines run efficiently, and our systems can handle production workloads.

But here's something we don't always pay enough attention to: Can we actually trust the data feeding these models?

After all, even the most sophisticated AI model can produce misleading results if the underlying data is incomplete, inconsistent, or incorrectly interpreted. What's interesting is that these problems don't always show up as technical failures. Sometimes, everything appears to be working perfectly fine.

Imagine a financial institution that has recently deployed an AI-powered loan risk assessment system. The team has spent months preparing the data, training the model, testing its performance, and finally moving it into production.

The deployment goes smoothly. Data pipelines are running on schedule, predictions are being generated, and all the monitoring dashboards are green.

A few weeks later, however, the business team notices something unusual. The model has started classifying more customers as high risk than expected.

The data science team checks the model configuration. Nothing has changed. The engineering team reviews the pipelines. All jobs have completed successfully. Even the infrastructure looks healthy.

It turns out that an upstream source system changed the definition of a customer income field from annual gross income to annual net income. The field name remained the same, the data type was still numeric, and none of the existing technical validations detected the change.

The pipeline continued running as expected, but the model was now receiving information with a different business meaning.

This brings us to an important point: Just because an AI pipeline runs successfully doesn't necessarily mean we can trust its output.

Building reliable AI systems requires us to look beyond the model itself. We need to understand where the data originates, how it changes as it moves through different systems, whether it meets quality expectations, and who is responsible when something goes wrong.

In this article, we'll explore three important elements of trustworthy AI pipelines (data quality, data lineage, and accountability) and look at how engineering teams can incorporate them into their existing workflows without making the process unnecessarily complicated.

Most modern data pipelines come with some form of operational monitoring. Engineering teams track job completion, execution time, system availability, and processing failures.

These metrics are important because they help us understand whether our systems are functioning as expected.

Operational monitoring primarily tells us whether a pipeline executed successfully. It doesn't necessarily tell us whether the data being processed is accurate, consistent, or suitable for the model consuming it.

Suppose we're building a loan risk model that uses a customer's debt-to-income ratio as one of its input features.

Debt-to-income ratio = Monthly debt payments / Monthly income

Now imagine that an upstream system starts supplying annual income instead of monthly income.

The calculation still executes successfully. The values are numeric, the transformation doesn't throw an error, and the model continues generating predictions.

However, the resulting debt-to-income ratio is incorrect because the numerator and denominator represent different time periods.

This is where traditional monitoring falls short.

To build trustworthy AI pipelines, we need to look at different dimensions of system health.

A pipeline can be operationally healthy while processing unreliable data. Similarly, a model can continue producing predictions even when the meaning of an important input field has changed.

For engineering teams, the objective should be to identify these problems as early as possible, ideally before they reach downstream models.

We've all heard the phrase "garbage in, garbage out."

And despite all the progress we've made in artificial intelligence, this principle hasn't changed.

AI models may be capable of processing enormous volumes of data and identifying complex patterns, but they cannot automatically guarantee that the information they're receiving is correct.

This is where data quality becomes important.

Data quality refers to how well information meets the requirements of its intended use. In AI pipelines, it helps us determine whether incoming data is sufficiently reliable for model training, evaluation, or inference.

Let's look at four dimensions that are particularly relevant.

Imagine we're developing a loan risk model that relies on customer income, outstanding debt, repayment history, and credit utilization.

What happens if 20% of the incoming records are missing income information?

Should we reject those records? Should we replace missing values using an approved statistical method? Or should we flag them for additional review?

There isn't one answer that works for every system.

The right approach depends on the model, the business requirements, and the potential consequences of the decision.

What matters is that missing information is handled deliberately.

For example, replacing missing income with zero may allow the pipeline to continue running, but it could significantly distort the model's predictions.

Validity checks whether values meet expected formats, ranges, and business rules.

Consider a customer record containing the following information:

The credit utilization value immediately stands out.

Although credit utilization can exceed 100% in certain circumstances, a value of 450% may warrant investigation.

This highlights an important distinction: Not every unusual value is incorrect, and not every technically valid value is meaningful.

A database may accept any numeric value, but the business may need additional rules to determine whether that value is appropriate for the model.

Engineering teams should distinguish between invalid records, unexpected observations, and legitimate exceptions.

This is one of the most common challenges in enterprise data environments.

Imagine two systems providing customer income information.

Both datasets contain a column called income.

If we combine these datasets without understanding their definitions, we risk introducing inconsistencies into downstream calculations.

The data may look perfectly fine from a technical perspective. The problem is that the same field represents different business concepts.

This is why data definitions, units, and transformation rules are just as important as schema validation.

Suppose a model predicts a customer's current financial risk using income information that was last updated 18 months ago.

The data might be accurate, complete, and consistent.

But is it current enough to support the decision?

Different AI applications have different freshness requirements.

A real-time fraud detection model may require near-real-time transaction information, while a long-term forecasting model may work with less frequently updated datasets.

The important thing is to define these expectations based on how the data will be used.

The good news is that improving data quality doesn't always require sophisticated enterprise tools.

A practical starting point is to define validation rules for the most important input fields.

For example, consider the following conceptual configuration:

This is an illustrative data quality contract rather than executable configuration for a particular framework.

It defines basic expectations and specifies what should happen when the data fails validation.

Tools such as Great Expectations can help automate these checks. For smaller systems, custom SQL or Python validations may be sufficient.

However, technical validation alone isn't enough.

Remember our original example where the income field changed from gross to net income?

Both values could easily pass a rule requiring income to be greater than zero.

Detecting that kind of issue requires clear business definitions, communication with upstream systems, and appropriate change management.

In other words, data quality isn't just about checking values. It's also about understanding what those values represent.

Now let's assume our loan risk model generates an unexpectedly high risk score for a customer.

The model output is available, but we want to understand what contributed to the prediction.

The income information came from a customer application system. Outstanding debt came from another database. Both datasets went through several transformations before reaching the feature engineering pipeline.

The final features were then passed to the model.

At this point, a seemingly simple question becomes surprisingly difficult:

Where exactly did the information used for this prediction come from?

This is where data lineage becomes valuable.

Data lineage describes how data originates, moves, and changes across different systems.

For AI pipelines, it helps us understand the journey from raw source data to the features used by a model.

Source Systems → Data Transformation → Feature Engineering → Model Inference → Predictions

Each stage introduces dependencies that can affect downstream results.

Data lineage can be captured at different levels of detail.

Dataset-level lineage shows relationships between datasets.

Loan Applications → Cleaned Applications → Model Features

This helps us understand how datasets move through the overall architecture.

However, suppose the model's debt-to-income ratio suddenly changes.

Dataset-level lineage might tell us which datasets were involved, but it may not provide enough detail to identify the affected field.

That's where column-level lineage becomes useful.

debt_to_income_ratio = monthly_debt_payment / monthly_income

Column-level lineage can show that the resulting feature depends on two upstream attributes.

If one of those attributes changes, engineers can trace the dependency and investigate the impact.

This is especially valuable in environments where multiple reports, applications, and AI models depend on shared data sources.

Large organizations may use dedicated metadata management and lineage platforms.

However, smaller teams can start by capturing essential metadata during pipeline execution.

OpenLineage provides an open standard for collecting and exchanging lineage metadata across supported systems.

It's important to understand that lineage doesn't automatically guarantee data accuracy.

A pipeline can be perfectly documented and still contain incorrect transformation logic.

But when something goes wrong, lineage gives engineering teams a clearer starting point for investigation.

Instead of searching across multiple systems and trying to reconstruct dependencies manually, they can identify where data originated and how it changed along the way.

An AI model starts producing unexpected results.

The data science team checks the model and finds no issues.

The data engineering team confirms that the pipelines are running successfully.

The source-system team verifies that data was delivered according to its interface.

Everyone has completed their individual checks, but nobody has identified who should coordinate the investigation.

This is where accountability becomes important.

AI pipelines often involve multiple teams responsible for different stages of the data lifecycle. Without clearly defined ownership, even relatively simple issues can take longer to resolve.

A practical accountability framework should distinguish between three responsibilities.

Data owners are responsible for defining the business meaning and expected quality of critical information.

For example, someone needs to determine whether an income field represents gross income, net income, or another financial measure.

These definitions should be documented and communicated to downstream consumers.

Data engineering and platform teams typically manage data ingestion, transformations, validation mechanisms, and pipeline operations.

Their responsibilities may include implementing quality checks, monitoring failures, and assessing the technical impact of changes.

However, technical teams shouldn't be expected to make business-definition decisions without appropriate input.

Data science and ML engineering teams are responsible for evaluating model behavior, managing model versions, and assessing how changes in input data may affect predictions.

If a critical feature changes, the model team may need to determine whether additional testing, recalibration, or retraining is required.

Defining ownership is useful, but connecting it to an operational process is even more important.

Consider a critical data quality failure during feature generation.

The exact workflow will vary depending on the organization and the risk associated with the application.

The objective is to ensure that technical alerts lead to clear actions.

After all, an alert that nobody owns is just another notification waiting to be ignored.

So far, we've discussed why data quality, lineage, and accountability matter.

But how can we bring these concepts into everyday engineering practices?

Here are five controls that teams can introduce without redesigning their entire architecture.

Data contracts define expectations between data producers and consumers.

They can document schemas, field definitions, units, freshness requirements, and compatibility expectations.

For example, rather than specifying only that annual_income is numeric, a contract should clarify whether it represents gross or net income, the currency, and the applicable time period.

This reduces the risk of downstream systems misinterpreting technically valid information.

Quality gates validate critical data before it progresses through the pipeline.

These checks can be implemented during ingestion, transformation, feature generation, or inference.

For example, a feature engineering process calculating debt-to-income ratios should verify that debt and income values use compatible time periods.

Failure handling should reflect the severity of the issue. Some records may be quarantined, while more serious failures may require stopping the pipeline.

Pipeline execution should produce sufficient metadata to identify source datasets, transformations, and downstream outputs.

Where appropriate, teams should also capture model and feature versions associated with predictions.

This information supports impact analysis, debugging, and investigation when unexpected results occur.

Datasets change, transformation logic is updated, features are modified, and models are retrained.

Versioning helps engineering teams understand which components were active at a particular point in time.

Relevant evidence may include pipeline configurations, dataset snapshots, model versions, validation outcomes, and deployment records.

Every critical data element or pipeline component should have an identified owner or responsible team.

More importantly, ownership should connect directly to incident management and change approval processes.

If a source definition changes, teams should know who evaluates the impact.

If a quality gate fails, the responsible team should receive the alert.

If model performance deteriorates, the investigation should have a clear escalation path.

These controls are most effective when they become part of normal development and deployment practices rather than separate administrative activities.

Implementing comprehensive governance across an entire data environment can be challenging, particularly for organizations with legacy systems and complex dependencies.

A more effective approach is to begin with one critical AI pipeline.

For example, a team operating a loan risk model could start by identifying the inputs that have the greatest influence on its predictions.

These might include income, outstanding debt, repayment history, and credit utilization.

For each critical input, the team can document five things:

Once these foundations are established, the team can implement automated checks and gradually extend the approach to additional datasets.

This incremental strategy also makes it easier to demonstrate the value of governance controls.

For instance, teams can track how quickly data-related incidents are detected, how long investigations take, and whether recurring issues are reduced.

The goal isn't to introduce governance for its own sake.

It's to improve the reliability, maintainability, and transparency of the systems developers are responsible for operating.

Explainable AI has become an important topic in machine learning.

Techniques such as feature importance analysis and SHAP can help teams understand which inputs influence model predictions.

However, explainability and data trustworthiness address different problems.

Suppose a model explanation identifies debt-to-income ratio as a major contributor to a customer's high-risk classification.

That explanation may accurately describe the model's behavior.

But if the debt-to-income ratio was calculated using an incorrectly defined income field, the explanation alone may not reveal the underlying data issue.

This highlights the importance of evaluating AI systems across multiple dimensions.

Model explainability helps us understand how predictions are generated.

Data quality helps establish whether inputs satisfy defined expectations.

Data lineage provides visibility into where information originated and how it was transformed.

Accountability ensures that identified problems can be investigated and addressed.

These capabilities are complementary rather than interchangeable.

None independently guarantees that an AI system is accurate, fair, secure, or compliant. Together, however, they provide a stronger foundation for evaluating and improving system reliability.

As AI systems become more integrated into business processes, engineering teams face increasing expectations around reliability, transparency, and accountability.

Model accuracy and operational performance remain essential, but they represent only part of the overall picture.

A pipeline can execute successfully while processing inconsistent information. A model can generate predictions using features whose definitions have changed. And an explanation can describe a model's behavior without identifying problems in the underlying data.

Building trustworthy AI therefore requires attention to the entire data lifecycle.

Data quality controls help detect unreliable inputs. Lineage provides visibility into how information moves through systems. Clear ownership ensures that issues can be investigated and resolved.

Importantly, these practices don't need to begin with large-scale governance programs or expensive technology investments.

Engineering teams can start with a single critical pipeline, identify its most important data elements, establish quality expectations, and introduce basic traceability and accountability mechanisms.

Over time, these practices can become part of standard development and deployment workflows.

The real measure of a trustworthy AI pipeline isn't simply whether it produces a prediction. It's whether the organization can understand, validate, and take responsibility for the information behind that prediction.

After all, a green dashboard is reassuring. But it shouldn't be the only evidence that an AI system is working as intended.

#10053. Wyze’s new window camera has a bird feeder and can identify species, more for $70 [Video]

#10049. YouTube appears to be quietly testing a new mute button in its video UI

Users are seeing the option to mute a video appear in the YouTube app on Android, but it isn’t showing up for everyone.

Screenshots of a new video control button have started appearing on Reddit over the last week. The posts haven’t exactly begun hot debates, but it’s an interesting UI change. A mute button now sits next to the fullscreen option in the left section of the video player. Tapping it does nothing other than cut audio, until it’s tapped again to unmute.

One user details the app version on their Pixel 11 Pro XL – version 21.46.522. They also note that other devices they’ve checked on the YouTube app versions don’t appear to show any sort of mute button in the UI. Without many comments on either post, it’s hard to nail down just how wide this rollout might be.

In reality, this seems like a very small UI design test for the YouTube app. YouTube will often select a small number of users to push experimental features to while they polish them further before release. Unlike features that Premium users get to test, these are locked.

It’s not necessarily a big change, but it’s sort of a confusing one. One wonders why a mute button is necessary. It also adds another button to accidentally hit when trying to tap the fullscreen option.

What do you think? If you’re seeing a mute button in the YouTube app, let us know your thoughts and what kind of phone you’re using.

Multiple employees from both OpenAI and Anthropic are sounding the alarm on the AI labs’ latest models. According to Axios, researchers are still looking for ways to contain and control unreleased models as they become more powerful, leading to debates about whether these models should be released publicly or if they should remain for internal use only. Some people are also arguing that it’s becoming impossible to keep the latest AI leashed and that companies should stop their development immediately.

“If we continue trying to make models much smarter, such that they’re actually smarter than us... they will in fact be able to trick us successfully,” a researcher told the publication. “And once you get to that point, many of the schemes that we’ve had at work up until now sort of go out the window.” There have previously been various incidents where unreleased models were able to break out of their testing environments and hack their way around the internet.

The report, which claims to have more than two dozen sources, cites "multiple" employees at frontier AI companies who worry their execs will choose profits over safety when it comes to releasing AI models. Furthermore, "they feel executives have become out of touch with the severity of safety risks."

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has even warned about an even more devastating occurrence, saying that an AI-driven botnet “swarm” could take over the internet in less than a year. The company went as far as listing “existential risks to humanity” as one of its risk factors in its IPO prospectus, although it has ironically proposed an AI safety bill that only kicks in if 50 deaths or $1 billion in damages are on the line.

Jensen Huang, the CEO of the company behind some of the hardware that enabled these breakthroughs in AI, said that “we have to shut the labs down” if these AI models prove to be unsafe, and that the civil and criminal liabilities that labs and developers would incur if AI models cause damage would be “incredible.” Still, he said that these fears are nothing but a distraction and not a call for regulation. A former OpenAI safety employee also said that the company’s safety culture is inherently broken and is calling for Silicon Valley to rely on safety experts from nuclear engineering and aviation to help “ensure that no single machine failure or human error would result in tragedy.”

A few months after the news of AI models escaping their testing environments, the White House convened the United States’ top AI tech executives, where they promised to “self-police” AI development and take steps “to build a positive future.” Despite that, a Democrat congresswoman has called for a ban on artificial superintelligence, echoing Sen. Bernie Sanders’ proposal to put a 20-year prison term on anyone caught developing the technology, and calling artificial intelligence an “out-of-control” industry.

#10045. Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

#10043. How Developers Can Detect Fake Payment Gateways and Build Secure Checkout Integrations

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Online payments are an essential part of modern web applications. E-commerce platforms, subscription services, and online marketplaces all depend on payment gateways to process transactions and manage customer orders.

However, integrating a payment gateway involves more than adding a payment button or displaying a checkout form. Developers must ensure that payment requests are sent to legitimate providers, transaction information is validated correctly, and payment confirmations cannot be manipulated.

Fake payment gateways introduce another challenge. They can imitate legitimate payment interfaces, use copied checkout templates, and redirect customers to fraudulent websites. Even when a checkout page looks professional, its underlying implementation may not be trustworthy.

For developers, understanding these risks is important when designing payment workflows. This article explores common payment integration vulnerabilities and practical techniques for building secure checkout systems using JavaScript and Node.js.

A payment gateway connects an application with a payment processing service. It handles transaction requests and communicates payment results to the merchant's application.

A typical checkout process involves several components:

A frontend checkout interface where customers review their orders.

A backend application that creates payment requests.

A payment provider that processes transactions.

A webhook endpoint that receives payment notifications.

A database that stores order and transaction information.

Attackers can target different parts of this process.

For example, a fraudulent website may copy the appearance of a legitimate checkout page while sending submitted information to an unauthorized server. Another approach involves manipulating payment requests or creating fake confirmation pages that make an unsuccessful transaction appear complete.

These techniques are also relevant to marketplace fraud. An eBay scam can involve fraudulent payment requests and misleading transaction links, illustrating why developers need to consider payment security beyond the appearance of a checkout page.

The important distinction is that a legitimate-looking interface does not establish that the payment request is reaching an authorized provider.

One of the first steps in reviewing a payment integration is checking where the application sends its payment requests.

Developers should inspect network requests using browser developer tools. The Network tab can reveal which domains receive requests when a customer opens the checkout page or submits a payment form.

Look for unexpected requests, unfamiliar domains, and scripts loaded from sources that are not part of the approved integration.

For example, a checkout application might load a payment script like this:

The URL above is illustrative, not an actual payment provider.

Developers should compare the script URL and integration method with the provider's official documentation rather than trusting a URL simply because it appears familiar.

Reviewing external JavaScript dependencies.

Checking whether payment forms submit data to approved endpoints.

Confirming that HTTPS is used throughout the payment process.

Reviewing changes to payment-related scripts during development.

A Content Security Policy (CSP) can also restrict which domains are allowed to load scripts and receive certain types of requests.

These example domains must be replaced with the actual domains documented by the selected payment provider.

A carefully configured policy can reduce the risk of unauthorized scripts and unexpected form submissions.

A common mistake in payment development is trusting values submitted directly from the browser.

Consider an application where the frontend sends an order ID and payment amount to the backend.

An attacker could modify the amount before submitting the request. If the backend accepts that value without checking the original order, the application may create a payment session for an incorrect amount.

The safer approach is to retrieve the order from the database and calculate the amount on the server.

Here, payment Gateway create Checkout Session() represents a provider-specific server-side SDK method.

The important part is that the backend retrieves the amount from its own database rather than accepting a price supplied by the customer.

Developers should also validate product IDs, quantities, discounts, currency, and order ownership before creating a payment session.

This prevents frontend manipulation from directly determining the final transaction amount.

Developers should avoid building payment processing around unverified scripts, copied templates, or unknown third-party services.

Instead, use official APIs and SDKs provided by established payment processors.

A hosted checkout integration is one common option. The merchant's backend creates a payment session through the provider's API, and the customer is redirected to the provider's hosted checkout page.

This approach can reduce the amount of sensitive card information handled by the merchant's application.

However, developers still need to configure the integration correctly.

Store secret API keys in server-side environment variables or a secrets manager.

Never expose private credentials in frontend JavaScript.

Use the provider's official SDK where available.

Validate checkout session creation on the backend.

Restrict success and cancellation URLs to approved destinations.

Use HTTPS for application and payment communication.

Developers should also understand the difference between public and secret API credentials. Public keys may be designed for browser use, while secret keys must remain on the server.

The OWASP Third-Party Payment Gateway Integration Cheat Sheet provides additional guidance on securing payment requests, validating transaction details, and handling payment callbacks.

Payment providers often use webhooks to notify applications when transactions are completed, failed, or refunded.

However, webhook endpoints are publicly accessible URLs in many integrations. Without proper verification, an attacker may send a fabricated HTTP request that looks like a successful payment notification.

Developers should verify webhook signatures using the payment provider's official SDK.

Consider this simplified Node.js example:

The verify Webhook() method represents the signature verification functionality of a payment provider's SDK. It is not a universal Node.js method.

The example also assumes that the provider's event contains the relevant order and transaction details. Developers should follow the provider's actual event schema and retrieve the authoritative payment status when required.

The application should never mark an order as paid simply because a customer has reached a success URL.

Instead, payment confirmation should depend on an authenticated provider notification or a server-side verification request.

Another important issue in payment development is duplicate processing.

Imagine a customer clicks the payment button twice because the first request takes too long. The application may create two payment sessions or attempt to charge the customer more than once.

Network interruptions and automatic retries can create similar situations.

Idempotency helps prevent these problems by ensuring that repeated requests with the same idempotency key do not create duplicate operations.

A simplified example using Node.js might look like this:

The example uses a provider-specific idempotency option. Actual SDK syntax and key requirements vary between providers.

In a production application, developers should also store payment attempts in the database and enforce appropriate uniqueness constraints.

This helps maintain consistent transaction records even when requests are repeated or interrupted.

A payment integration should be tested beyond the normal successful checkout process.

Most established payment providers offer sandbox environments that allow developers to simulate transactions without charging real customers.

These environments are useful for testing different transaction scenarios.

Developers should also test whether the application handles provider downtime correctly.

For example, if the payment provider becomes temporarily unavailable, the application should not mark an order as completed or assume that the transaction succeeded.

Automated integration tests can help verify these scenarios before deployment. Monitoring payment failures, unexpected status changes, and repeated webhook errors can also help developers identify problems after release.

Security does not end when a payment integration goes live.

Developers should implement monitoring to identify unusual transaction patterns and unexpected changes in payment behavior.

Recording payment attempts and transaction IDs.

Monitoring webhook signature verification failures.

Detecting repeated requests for the same order.

Alerting on unexpected order status changes.

However, logging must be implemented carefully.

Applications should never store card verification codes or expose secret API keys in application logs. Sensitive information should be excluded from debugging output, and access to transaction records should be restricted.

Developers should also follow the payment provider's security requirements and applicable PCI DSS obligations when handling payment data.

Detecting fake payment gateways and building secure checkout integrations requires more than checking whether a payment page looks legitimate.

Developers need to examine the complete transaction flow, validate payment information on the server, use official payment APIs, verify webhook signatures, and prevent duplicate transactions.

JavaScript and Node.js provide useful tools for implementing these security controls, but the reliability of a payment system ultimately depends on how its frontend, backend, database, and payment provider communicate.

A well-designed checkout integration should verify every important transaction before fulfilling an order. By following these development practices, teams can build payment systems that are easier to maintain, more reliable, and better protected against common payment manipulation techniques.

#10042. Yes, the Pixel 10 is still a great buy [Video]

If you’re looking for a decent replacement for an older phone or a backup, and the Pixel 10 looks eerily similar to the Pixel 11, is it still worth it? Well, of course it is, but let’s get into why in this deep dive.

One of the best things about the Pixel 10 is that it looks almost identical to the Pixel 11; in some cases, it’ll work with the accessories for the newest phone – but check that before you try. So that means you’re not getting a majorly out-of-date design.

That doesn’t matter to most people, but it could be important to you. Pixelsnap magnetic accessories still work, and while the Pixel 11 trimmed down the camera bar to an all-glass visor, the overall footprint is virtually identical.

Using the same formula might be stale to some people, but it works, so why change it for the sake of it?

I’d argue that the best news is that this finally shows Google is confident, given its success with the Pixel 9 and 10 design. The company may make changes in the future, but it’s a fairly solid design that feels great after they settled on a physical form factor that works after years of fiddling, and it’s no secret that the goal is to make the switch from iOS to Android as “easy and painless” as possible. A familiar design goes a long way to calming the nerves of a potential switcher. The Pixel 10 is a perfect example of this philosophy. The Pixel 11 essentially proves that too.

Over a year on from release, there don’t seem to be any major or widespread reports of issues on the Pixel 10. I’d say that is a good omen, as people will often know within that timeframe if there are quality control issues and problematic hardware faults.

That said, the only problem I think you have to deal with is the base 128GB storage configuration. It’s not insignificant, but still not going to be tons of room for everything. If you take lots of photos and download loads of things locally to your device, then maybe go for the 256GB model.

One of the weirdest things about the Pixel 10 was that after the Pixel 11 launched along with all other supported handsets, it had a newer version of Android than the latest phone.

Yes, you read that correctly: the Pixel 10 had QPR1 before the Pixel 11.

The entire experience is just more of the same goodness that started with the addition of Material 3 Expressive last year. Android 17 keeps improving; the quality-of-life changes make for a really compelling experience that, while taking some cues from iOS, hasn’t gone full Liquid Glass. Google hasn’t quite kept the day-one update promise like the old days of Pixel, but you still have the cutting edge of the software. Even the Galaxy S26 series isn’t running a release this new and probably won’t for a while.

Rambler has even been added recently, so there is a function bleeding down from the Pixel 11, and there are loads of other quality-of-life changes and features that came with Android 17 QPR1 thrown in. Any bugs that might have been there, however limited, have been addressed in the year the phone has been around.

With the Pixel 10, you may miss out on some of the extra Gemini Intelligence perks or a couple of visual gimmicks like the Pro’s HiLight glow, but none that is yet groundbreaking enough to be drastically upset about.

Sure, the competition might offer a faster processor or newer silicon, but can they offer the fluidity of a system that is fully integrated into all parts of your digital life? That’s what the Pixel 10 delivers.

As I noted, the Pixel 10 has already seen multiple updates, smoothing out early thermal quirks and optimizing battery efficiency. These monthly security patches and Pixel Drops are a testament to the fact that your phone is a constantly evolving product.

With more updates due very soon, the prospect of an even more optimized experience, and many of the best bits of Google all rolled into one handset, it’s a damn good buy, especially as other OEMs are still struggling to get their own Android 17 updates out in a timely fashion. We’re several months on from the full launch, and barely anyone outside of Samsung has offered a wide rollout. Of course, all Pixel 10 devices get day-one updates, with six years of support still left on the clock.

There is one sore point on the base Pixel 10 that I think might make it hard to recommend over the Pixel 10 Pro. The main camera is the same as the Pixel 9a and the 10a on the base 10. It makes it harder to suggest if you care about camera quality.

It’s still super reliable and does take great photos, but it might not be the best in class like previous generations may have been. In fact, I’d go so far as to say that while it’s great you get a telephoto zoom lens, the lower-quality main sensor diminishes it somewhat.

Most people will be better off with the Pro because it has a lot more to work with, but the cameras are still very good on the Pixel 10 and are backed by Google’s excellent updates.

Even video quality stacks up, though I will say Google’s reliance on Video Boost as a workaround for lower-quality footage is a bit of a cop-out. However, it’s a good workaround, and the resulting footage is still excellent, even if I’m hoping for better on-device processing in future Pixel generations.

Ultimately, the camera is a prime example of the Pixel value proposition. You don’t need the absolute latest, greatest, multi-lens Pro Max Ultra setup to take amazing photos. You need a Pixel. The camera on the 10 series is still right up there with the best from Samsung, OnePlus, and others. It’s doing just as much with technically much less.

The biggest reason that many people who are looking for a new phone might want to consider at least the Pixel 10 lineup is that, with discounts, you’re getting a great deal — especially since Google bumped the entry price on the Pixel 11. Sure, bigger, better, faster, and shinier phones exist. They don’t give you the same experience, though. You’re paying for something that others can’t replicate, even at higher price tags.

With more updates due very soon, the prospect of an even more optimized experience, and many of the best bits of Google all rolled into one handset, it’s a damn good buy.

Is the Pixel 10 the best option for a phone right now? Suppose you want arguably the best software and are willing to forgo a few of the bells and whistles. In that case, it is an excellent alternative for anyone looking to save a little money without sacrificing too much. It’s a better buy than the Pixel 10a for most people, in my opinion, and you’ll be hard-pressed to find a more compelling mix of software intelligence and hardware value.

#10040. Lenovo Legion Go Beam leaks with Android instead of SteamOS

The variety of gaming handhelds from Lenovo is about to get larger. Leaked details suggest the Lenovo Legion Go Beam will run Android instead of SteamOS on a high-refresh display.

Lenovo is sticking with the Legion Go branding to offer something a little different to users. Instead of a SteamOS or Windows device, the Legion Go Beam is said to run Android (via Windows Latest). Specifically, Android 16.

That doesn’t limit it to Android titles alone. Android happens to make the perfect platform for cloud game streaming. Services like GeForce Now, Xbox Game Pass, and more offer a comparable experience to what a PC does for a fixed subscription, and that’s without the hardware internals something like the Legion Go 2 would need to run games natively.

The leak also notes the Lenovo Legion Go Beam will have a 7-inch 120Hz display, though there’s no information on what resolution or panel type the handheld might have. It’s likely 1080p or higher, though a lower resolution obviously will net users better performance. Battery is up in the air, too, other than a standard “long-lasting battery life” claim attributed to Lenovo.

This handheld takes a lot from the Legion Go 2 in design, as it should. It’s a comfortable device, and this shouldn’t be any different. It lacks a trackpad in favor of a traditional controller layout. It also has standard ports like one USB-C and 3.5mm headphone jack.

Pricing is a mystery. Devices like the Logitech G Cloud were such a hit because they delivered the needed performance at a good price. But those handhelds had a lower refresh rate. Still, hoping for a price tag around $350 or less isn’t such a bad thing.

#10035. What to Check Before Adding a Tripo Model to a Website

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A 3D model can look convincing in a still image and still be awkward on a website. The real questions appear when someone rotates it, loads it on a typical connection, or views it on a narrow phone screen. AI generators have made the first draft of a model cheap, which shifts the work toward the part developers and designers already know: budgets, testing and fallbacks.

This article walks through a web-readiness review for an AI-generated model, using an openwork ceramic vessel generated from a single reference image with Tripo as the worked example. The checks apply equally to output from other generators. It includes a small Node.js script that audits a GLB file against a size budget, and the markup for a lightweight viewer with a static fallback. The model shown is one candidate, so it cannot prove browser speed, mobile behavior, hidden geometry or export compatibility. Those are checks to run on your own asset.

Traditional 3D work involves modeling, polygon layout, materials, scale and export. AI generation can shorten the blank-workspace stage. A beginner may discover the category through a search such as AI 3D model generator free, but an initial candidate is useful only if it survives review.

For this vessel, start with the negative space, meaning the openings between the ribs. A flattering front view can hide a blocked cavity, a thin wall, or two surfaces passing through each other. Generation moves the bottleneck from making a first shape to deciding whether that shape is usable.

Image-to-3D tools use a visual reference to create a model candidate, while text-to-3D routes start from a written prompt. Tripo offers both, and other hosted options include Meshy, Rodin by Hyper3D and 3D AI Studio, all of which list GLB among their export formats, while open-source models such as TripoSR and Stable Fast 3D let you run generation yourself. Features, pricing, licensing and output quality differ and change often, so check each vendor’s current documentation. The image route suits this example because the folds, uneven rim and openings already exist in the source.

Whichever tool you use, it can provide a candidate for inspection. It cannot decide whether the source is licensed, whether the unseen rear structure is believable, or whether the exported asset suits a particular viewer. Those judgments belong to the person publishing the page.

Give the object one job. In a portfolio, this vessel might show how its shell and openings relate. That is narrower than calling it printable, manufacturable or dimensionally accurate. Note what must remain recognizable, and provide a still image if the interactive view fails.

Use an image you own or have permission to reuse. Busy backgrounds can distract an image-based workflow, so isolate the object when possible. One photograph also leaves the back and underside unknown. Treat those areas as review risks, not as details confirmed by the source.

After generation, rotate beyond the familiar front. Check the rear, top, underside and sides. Trace each large opening. Look for merged ribs, intersecting surfaces or an unstable base. A wireframe view, when available, can expose crowded geometry. A single front-facing candidate cannot answer these hidden-angle questions, so check them on your own model.

Record the download size, polygon count and texture dimensions. Polygons are the small flat faces that form the shape, and web viewers draw them as triangles. This matters because generator defaults are often tuned for quality, not for the web. In the generation screenshot for this vessel, the viewer reports roughly 1.95 million faces and the settings show 4K texture quality. That is a fine starting point for inspection but a heavy asset for a phone on a mobile connection. Curved ribs may need many triangles, but the count should still fit your page budget.

glTF is the open format most web viewers expect, and GLB is its single-file form that can bundle geometry, textures and materials. The format does not guarantee good performance, so measure the package. The script below reads a GLB with the open-source glTF Transform library, counts triangles, lists texture sizes, and compares them with a budget. The budget values are placeholders; set your own from your target devices.

Run it with node audit.mjs model.glb after installing @gltf-transform/core. It exits with a non-zero status when the asset is over budget, so you can run it in CI. A short test builds two GLBs with known triangle counts, one small and one with 200,000 triangles, and checks that the first passes and the second fails with the right message:

When an asset fails, glTF Transform also has a command line. gltf-transform inspect model.glb reports what is inside a file, and gltf-transform optimize input.glb output.glb --compress draco --texture-compress webp applies geometry and texture compression. Compression and mesh simplification can introduce artifacts, especially around thin ribs and openings, so reopen the result and re-check the angles from Gate 3 instead of accepting a smaller file blindly. Also check that your viewer supports the compression you choose.

Load the asset in the intended viewer at desktop and phone widths. Check framing, touch rotation, keyboard access, scrolling and a slower connection. The Google-backed model-viewer web component is one low-code option, and libraries such as three.js give you more control at the cost of more code. The markup below shows the pieces that matter for a first release: a poster image that loads first, lazy loading so the heavy file waits until needed, a text alternative, and visible controls.

The alt text, a poster and touch-action="pan-y" (so a vertical swipe still scrolls the page on a phone) address the common failures. Check the current model-viewer documentation for attribute names and the recommended script include, since they can change between versions. Also consider the WCAG guidance on non-text content: provide a useful description, and offer instructions plus a static image for people who cannot operate the viewer.

A single front-facing candidate is enough to discuss the method, not to certify a finished web asset. Call it a prototype until the exported file passes the checks required by its actual destination.

The same review pattern can be adapted to an architectural ornament, exhibition object, packaging concept or game prop, but each destination adds its own geometry, scale and delivery requirements. A portfolio may accept an inferred rear surface if that limitation is clear. A sales page, printable file or measured product needs stronger references and technical validation.

The audit script counts triangles, texture sizes and file size only. It does not detect self-intersections, thin walls, flipped normals or licensing problems, and passing it does not make an asset good. The budget numbers are illustrative, and real limits depend on your audience’s devices and connections. Tool features and terms change, so verify them with the vendor before you rely on them, and treat any speed or quality claims on vendor pages as the vendor’s own.

Inspect the angle you would normally crop out. Confirm input rights, measure the download, and test touch, keyboard and a slow connection. Reread the surrounding copy, because claims of fabrication accuracy or production readiness need supporting evidence. For this vessel, “exploratory web model” is an honest label, and more demanding claims require better references and a specialist review.

Decide what visitors should learn or do before adding a 3D viewer. A modest prototype with a useful fallback can beat a dramatic asset that loads slowly or hides limitations.

Do I need to code to create a model with an AI generator? No code is required to generate an initial model candidate in Tripo or similar tools. Publishing it interactively is separate work and usually involves a viewer, hosting, responsive testing and enough HTML or JavaScript knowledge to diagnose problems.

When should I use image-to-3D instead of text-to-3D? Use image-to-3D when a rights-cleared visual should guide the form, and text-to-3D when the idea begins as words. Do not treat the two routes as interchangeable evidence.

Is a generated model ready to publish as soon as it exports? No. Inspect the shape from several angles, measure the delivery package, test the intended viewer and prepare a fallback. Higher-stakes uses may also need specialist geometry, scale, material and rights review.

How can a beginner keep web 3D manageable? Give one object one clear job. Set a file-size target, test desktop and phone widths, keep interaction simple, and retain a still image for readers who cannot load or operate the viewer.

#10033. US DOJ touts "largest-ever" crackdown on scammers across the globe as it seizes 14,000 devices and $17 billion in illicit funds

More than 400 criminal cases have been filed across 85 federal districts in the U.S. alone.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The United States Department of Justice announced today that more than 800 defendants have been charged and over $8 billion in intended victim losses have been recovered in what it describes as the "largest ever" crackdown on scammers and fraudsters in its history. The charges have led to over 400 cases, filed across 85 federal districts, with outcomes including extraditions, guilty pleas, and prison sentences for those involved.

"We are not just going after individual scammers," Fox News reports U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche as saying. "We are dismantling the criminal networks that have been stealing from the American people for far too long."

The push follows President Donald Trump's executive order from March, which required a government-wide crackdown on the kinds of fraud and predatory schemes that people fall victim to each and every day across the country.

FBI Director Kash Patel also gave details of Operation Blackout, an interagency project that targets international fraudsters running centralized facilities. Patel said that FBI agents, alongside their international counterparts, have dismantled 29 centralized compounds across Asia, Africa, and the Middle East. The department claims those agents have seized almost $17 billion in funds, that more than 14,000 involved devices have been confiscated, and that 50 "scam actors" have been brought up on U.S. federal charges. The FBI also says its support allowed its foreign partners to arrest or detain more than 320 criminals in foreign jurisdictions.

Perhaps most importantly, these compounds often use human trafficking victims to run online scams. Patel claims that more than 2,200 trafficked workers have been freed as a result of the initiative.

Global scams and hacking are an ongoing problem for the United States and its citizens, not to mention its businesses, big and small. Just last month, more than $5 million in gift cards were stolen from Staples before being used at retailers like Target and Lowes. A Chinese crime ring was behind that attack, which involved more than 15,000 cards.

Earlier this year, a Chinese phishing service was dismantled after it used AI tools to build scam websites linked to almost $2 billion in losses. An October report even detailed a computer, hidden behind a Japanese hotel room fridge, that was responsible for more than 480,000 phishing emails.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The problem is only likely to get worse as the capabilities and availability of AI tools continue to expand. U.S. banks have scrambled to restrict AI agent activity over concerns about what they could do to customer accounts. Some banks raised the alarm after detecting agents accessing customer accounts and even attempting to process transactions. Morgan Stanley, Bank of America, JPMorgan, and Wells Fargo are among those that have blocked the agents while updating their terms of service accordingly.

That growing threat only emphasizes the fact that the department has much work left to do. The $17 billion seized by the FBI is only a portion of the estimated $68 billion Americans lost to scams in 2025, according to Gallup data, so the ongoing game of cat and mouse between the government and criminal enterprises the world over will only continue.

#10031. This $199 Amazon TV is an easy choice for your guest bedroom

This Amazon Ember TV Black Friday deal takes up to 40% off the company’s own TV brand, making it ideal for a secondary set.

Amazon Fire TVs (now known as Amazon Ember TVs) are typically quite affordable and, of course, Black Friday week is making those prices even better than usual. Case in point, the company’s most affordable TV is usually $329, but that same model is down to an incredible $199, making it ridiculously easy to recommend no matter how picky you might be about your TVs. And yes, this is the 2026 model, so you’re not buying something ancient.

I have no idea why Amazon is so obsessed with fire-related names (Kindle, Fire TV, Ember, etc.), but it’s hard to ignore the company’s leanings toward value-heavy products that cost very little to acquire. The Amazon Ember TV lineup sports 4K resolution, support for HDR10, HDR10+, and HLG, Dolby Audio, an ambient artwork screensaver, and lots of free streaming content built-in. It’s even got Amazon Luna built in, which gives you games like Star Wars Galactic Racer for free.

All three models ship with the latest Fire TV experience and Alexa+, which is a significantly smarter, LLM-powered version of Alexa (Alexa GPT, basically). It has apps for all the major streaming networks and even YouTube, but these don’t have the Google Play Store or a Google TV app, so keep that in mind before buying.

Of course, a $199 TV will not have the same image quality as a $600-plus TV, but that’s probably not an issue if this is just a secondary TV for a spare bedroom (or a backup TV).

#10027. TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.
